 going to look at the anatomy of a Java program. In this Java tutorial

[00:04:04.460–00:04:08.600] **SPEECH**
 we're going to look at the anatomy of Java programs. The smallest building block

[00:04:08.600–00:04:12.440] **SPEECH**
 in Java programs are functions. A function is a block of code

[00:04:12.440–00:04:16.600] **SPEECH**
 that performs a task. As a metaphor, think of the buttons on the remote

[00:04:16.600–00:04:20.340] **SPEECH**
 control of your TV. Each button performs a task. Functions

[00:04:20.340–00:04:24.340] **SPEECH**
 in programming languages are exactly the same. For example, we can have a function for

[00:04:24.340–00:04:28.640] **SPEECH**
 sending emails to people, we can have a function for converting someone's weight

[00:04:28.640–00:04:32.420] **SPEECH**
 in pounds to kilograms, we can have a function for validating users

[00:04:32.420–00:04:36.020] **SPEECH**
 input and so on. Now let's see how we can code a function in java.

[00:04:36.660–00:04:40.540] **SPEECH**
 We start by specifying the return type of that function. Some functions

[00:04:40.540–00:04:44.800] **SPEECH**
 return a value like a number, a day time and so on, other functions don't return

[00:04:44.800–00:04:47.980] **SPEECH**
 anything. So the return type of these functions is void.

[00:04:48.680–00:04:52.940] **SPEECH**
 Void is a reserved keyword in java and that's why I've coded that in blue here.

[00:04:52.940–00:04:57.560] **SPEECH**
 Now, after the return type, we have the name of our function. So here we should give our

[00:04:57.560–00:05:01.020] **SPEECH**
 function a proper descriptive name, like send

[00:05:01.020–00:05:05.280] **SPEECH**
 email. This name clearly identifies the purpose of this function,

[00:05:05.940–00:05:09.060] **SPEECH**
 okay. Now after the name we have a pair of parentheses

[00:05:09.060–00:05:13.360] **SPEECH**
 and inside this parentheses we add the parameters for this function.

[00:05:13.920–00:05:16.820] **SPEECH**
 We use these parameters to pass values to our function.

[00:05:17.320–00:05:21.080] **SPEECH**
 For example, our send email function should have parameters like who is the receiver,

[00:05:21.080–00:05:25.200] **SPEECH**
 what is the subject of this email, what is the content of this email, and so on.

[00:05:25.700–00:05:28.980] **SPEECH**
 Now in this tutorial we're not going to worry about parameters, we'll look at them in the future.

[00:05:30.060–00:05:33.000] **SPEECH**
 Now after the parenthesis we add a pair of curly braces

[00:05:33.000–00:05:36.320] **SPEECH**
 and inside these braces we write the actual

[00:05:36.320–00:05:40.540] **SPEECH**
 Java code. Now one thing I want you to pay attention to here is that in Java

[00:05:41.020–00:05:44.940] **SPEECH**
 we put the left brace on the same line where we define our function.

[00:05:45.660–00:05:49.240] **SPEECH**
 In other programming languages like C sharp, it's more conventional

[00:05:49.240–00:05:53.400] **SPEECH**
 to put the left brace on a new line, but we don't do that in Java. So

[00:05:53.400–00:05:57.020] **SPEECH**
 we put the left brace on the same line where we define our function.

[00:05:57.900–00:06:01.020] **SPEECH**
 Now, every Java program should have at least one function,

[00:06:01.400–00:06:05.260] **SPEECH**
 and that function is called main. So main is the entry point

[00:06:05.260–00:06:09.440] **SPEECH**
 to our programs. Whenever we execute a Java program, the main

[00:06:09.440–00:06:13.080] **SPEECH**
 function gets called, and the code inside this function gets executed.

[00:06:13.660–00:06:17.320] **SPEECH**
 Okay? Now, these functions don't exist on their own. They should always

[00:06:17.320–00:06:21.500] **SPEECH**
 belong to a class. So a class is a container for

[00:06:21.500–00:06:25.480] **SPEECH**
 one or more related functions. Basically we use these classes to organize

[00:06:25.480–00:06:28.940] **SPEECH**
 our code. Just like how products are organized in a supermarket.

[00:06:29.620–00:06:33.160] **SPEECH**
 In a supermarket we have various sections like vegetables, fruits,

[00:06:33.600–00:06:37.640] **SPEECH**
 cleaning products, and so on. Each section contains related products.

[00:06:38.180–00:06:41.440] **SPEECH**
 By the same token, a class in Java contains related

[00:06:41.440–00:06:45.560] **SPEECH**
 functions. Now, every Java program should have at least one class

[00:06:45.560–00:06:49.540] **SPEECH**
 that contains the main function. Can you guess the name of that class?

[00:06:50.480–00:06:53.640] **SPEECH**
 It's main. So this is how we define a class in java.

[00:06:54.260–00:06:57.740] **SPEECH**
 We start with a class keyword, then we give our class a proper

[00:06:57.740–00:07:01.100] **SPEECH**
 descriptive name, and then we add a pair of curly braces.

[00:07:01.760–00:07:05.720] **SPEECH**
 Now the functions that we define in between these curly braces belong to

[00:07:05.720–00:07:09.400] **SPEECH**
 this class, and more accurately we refer to them as methods.

[00:07:10.040–00:07:13.600] **SPEECH**
 So a method is a function that is part of a class. In some

[00:07:13.600–00:07:18.060] **SPEECH**
 languages like python, we can have a function that exists outside

[00:07:18.060–00:07:22.220] **SPEECH**
 of a class, so we call it a function, but when a function belongs to a class

[00:07:22.220–00:07:26.120] **SPEECH**
 we refer to it as a method of that class, okay? Now

[00:07:26.120–00:07:30.300] **SPEECH**
 in Java, all these classes and methods should have an

[00:07:30.300–00:07:33.940] **SPEECH**
 access modifier. An access modifier is a special keyword

[00:07:33.940–00:07:37.740] **SPEECH**
 that determines if other classes and methods in this program

[00:07:37.740–00:07:41.760] **SPEECH**
 can access these classes and methods. We have various access

[00:07:41.760–00:07:45.880] **SPEECH**
 modifiers like public, private, and so on. Now most of the time

[00:07:45.880–00:07:49.780] **SPEECH**
 we use the public access modifier, so we put that in front of our

[00:07:49.780–00:07:53.880] **SPEECH**
 class and method declarations. So this is the basic structure

[00:07:53.880–00:07:57.620] **SPEECH**
 of a Java program. At a minimum we have a main class

[00:07:57.620–00:08:01.860] **SPEECH**
 and inside this main class we have the main method. Now you might

[00:08:01.860–00:08:05.300] **SPEECH**
 be curious why we have a capital M in the name of this class.

[00:08:05.840–00:08:09.700] **SPEECH**
 Because in Java we use different conventions for naming our classes and our

[00:08:09.700–00:08:13.840] **SPEECH**
 to name our classes, we use the Pascal naming convention

[00:08:13.840–00:08:18.020] **SPEECH**
 and that basically means the first letter of every word should be upper case.

[00:08:18.580–00:08:22.340] **SPEECH**
 In contrast to name our methods, we use the camel naming convention

[00:08:22.340–00:08:26.000] **SPEECH**
 and that means the first letter of every word should be upper case

[00:08:26.000–00:08:30.100] **SPEECH**
 except the first word. So that is why we have a capital M

[00:08:30.100–00:08:33.880] **SPEECH**
 in the name of this class. Alright, now that you understand the anatomy

[00:08:33.880–00:08:38.160] **SPEECH**
 of a Java program, let's create a new Java project and see all these building

[00:08:38.160–00:08:47.750] **SPEECH**
 blocks in action. In this java tutorial, you're going to learn how to write and

 execute your first java program. So let's open IntelliJ

 idea. Here on the home screen, let's create a new project.

 Alright, on the left side, select java,

 and make sure project SDK is not blank. So earlier we downloaded

 JDK or java development kit version 12,

 that is why JDK version 12 is selected here. If you don't see that, make sure

 What is this? Well, earlier we talked about classes and methods.

 to other people, so these comments don't get executed, now let's

 remove this comment and write a bit of code to print something on the terminal

 so here we're going to use the system class in java

 so capital s system, here in this tool tip you can see

 the system class is defined in this package, java

 or language. Also look at this icon on the left side,

 this indicates a class. Now inside this class we have

 various members. We can use the dot operator to see the members

 defined in the system class. Now the member that we're going to access is

 out. Look at the icon of this member. It's

 F which is short for field. We're going to talk about fields in the future when we talk

 about classes and object oriented programming. Now what is interesting

 is the type of this field, and you can see that on the right side, that is print

 stream. So print stream is another class that is defined in

 java. So, once again we use the dot operator to look at

 the methods or members defined in the print stream class.

 The method we're going to use is print ln, which is short for line,

 look at the icon for this method, so m indicates a

 method. Now, you press enter, and

 IntelliJ automatically adds these parentheses as well as a semicolon.

 So now with the code on line 6, we're calling or executing

 and inside these quotes write hello word. So hello

[00:15:14.640–00:15:20.460] **SPEECH**
 word is textual data, in Java whenever we deal with textual

 with double quotes. Now we say we have a string, so

 a string is a sequence of characters. Alright, so we're done

 with our first program, now to execute this, we can click on this icon

 and r. I always prefer to use shortcuts because they're faster, so control

 and r. Now, intelligent is building

 our application, and we can see the result in this little terminal window.

 So here is our hello work message. So that was our first

[00:15:52.880–00:15:57.100] **SPEECH**
 Java program. Next I'm going to explain how Java code gets executed

 under the hood. Hey, Mosh here.

 Also, be sure to subscribe and enable the notifications,

 Thank you so much, and let's continue watching.

[00:16:30.770–00:16:33.630] **SPEECH**
 Alright, now let's see what exactly happens under the hood

[00:16:33.630–00:16:36.610] **SPEECH**
 the moment we run a Java program in IntelliJ.

[00:16:37.190–00:16:38.950] **SPEECH**
 There are basically two steps involved here.

[00:16:39.490–00:16:41.070] **SPEECH**
 Compilation and execution.

[00:16:41.810–00:16:45.070] **SPEECH**
 In the compilation step, IntelliJ uses the Java compiler

[00:16:45.070–00:16:48.090] **SPEECH**
 to compile our code into a different format called

[00:16:48.090–00:16:52.810] **SPEECH**
 Java bytecode. This Java compiler comes with the Java development kit

[00:16:52.810–00:16:56.030] **SPEECH**
 that we downloaded at the beginning of the course. Let me show you.

[00:16:56.890–00:17:00.650] **SPEECH**
 So here we can right click on this main.java, and in this

[00:17:00.650–00:17:04.410] **SPEECH**
 context menu, we have an item called open in terminal.

[00:17:04.970–00:17:08.050] **SPEECH**
 It's down below, unfortunately it's not visible in my recording window.

[00:17:08.470–00:17:12.590] **SPEECH**
 It's called open in terminal on mac, and probably open in command prompt

[00:17:12.590–00:17:15.870] **SPEECH**
 on windows. So, let's open that.

[00:17:15.870–00:17:19.150] **SPEECH**
 we get this terminal window or command prompt on windows,

[00:17:19.890–00:17:23.750] **SPEECH**
 here we're currently inside of this folder, code with mosh. That is where

[00:17:23.750–00:17:27.750] **SPEECH**
 we have our main.java file. Now we can invoke the java

[00:17:27.750–00:17:31.910] **SPEECH**
 compiler like this, java c, and pass the name of our java file

[00:17:31.910–00:17:35.910] **SPEECH**
 as an argument. So main, the java. If you're on mac

[00:17:35.910–00:17:39.850] **SPEECH**
 or linux, make sure to spell this with a capital m, because these operating systems

[00:17:39.850–00:17:43.890] **SPEECH**
 are case sensitive. So, enter, now let's

[00:17:43.890–00:17:47.190] **SPEECH**
 content of this folder. On Mac or Linux we can type

[00:17:47.190–00:17:52.510] **SPEECH**
 ls, on windows we type dir. So, let's take a look.

[00:17:52.950–00:17:56.830] **SPEECH**
 In this folder now we have a new file main.class, this is the

[00:17:56.830–00:18:00.870] **SPEECH**
 bytecode representation of this java file. Now, when we use

[00:18:00.870–00:18:04.370] **SPEECH**
 IntelliJ to run our java program, this class file gets

[00:18:04.370–00:18:08.470] **SPEECH**
 stored somewhere else. Let me show you. So back to the project panel,

[00:18:09.710–00:18:13.030] **SPEECH**
 here in our project, we have this source folder where we have our

[00:18:13.030–00:18:17.130] **SPEECH**
 source code, and we have this out folder where we have the result

[00:18:17.130–00:18:21.030] **SPEECH**
 of the compilation. So, inside this folder we have production,

[00:18:21.750–00:18:24.510] **SPEECH**
 inside this we have hello world, the same name as our project,

[00:18:25.290–00:18:28.690] **SPEECH**
 inside hello world we have com, which is the name of our top level package,

[00:18:29.770–00:18:33.170] **SPEECH**
 inside this package we have a sub package that is code with mosh,

[00:18:33.990–00:18:37.190] **SPEECH**
 and here we have our main.class file. So this was

[00:18:37.190–00:18:41.170] **SPEECH**
 the compilation step. Now this java byte code that we have in this file

[00:18:41.170–00:18:45.050] **SPEECH**
 is platform independent, and that means it can run on Windows,

[00:18:45.470–00:18:49.290] **SPEECH**
 Mac, Linux, or any operating systems that has a Java

[00:18:49.290–00:18:53.250] **SPEECH**
 runtime environment. If you go to java.com

[00:18:53.250–00:18:56.850] **SPEECH**
 slash download, we can download Java or more accurately

[00:18:56.850–00:19:01.090] **SPEECH**
 Java runtime environment for various operating systems. This Java

[00:19:01.090–00:19:04.610] **SPEECH**
 runtime environment has a software component called Java

[00:19:04.610–00:19:08.890] **SPEECH**
 Virtual Machine or JVM. This JVM takes our Java

[00:19:08.890–00:19:13.930] **SPEECH**
 and translates it to the native code for the underlying operating system.

[00:19:14.670–00:19:17.510] **SPEECH**
 So if you're on a windows machine, this java virtual machine

[00:19:17.510–00:19:21.890] **SPEECH**
 converts our java bytecode into the native code that windows can understand.

[00:19:22.550–00:19:26.030] **SPEECH**
 This architecture is the reason why java applications are portable

[00:19:26.030–00:19:29.970] **SPEECH**
 or platform independent. We can write a java program on a

[00:19:29.970–00:19:34.270] **SPEECH**
 windows machine and execute it on linux, mac, or any other operating

[00:19:34.270–00:19:38.870] **SPEECH**
 systems that have a java runtime environment. C sharp and

[00:19:38.870–00:19:42.610] **SPEECH**
 the same architecture. That's why they are platform independent as well.

[00:19:43.450–00:19:46.870] **SPEECH**
 Now let me show you how to invoke this Java virtual machine to run a

[00:19:46.870–00:19:51.010] **SPEECH**
 Java program. So back to this terminal window, let me

[00:19:51.010–00:19:54.630] **SPEECH**
 expand this. Currently we are inside of this folder

[00:19:54.630–00:19:59.070] **SPEECH**
 code with mosh, and in this folder we have this class file. Now let's go

[00:19:59.070–00:20:03.190] **SPEECH**
 one level up, so cd, dot dot, and one more time.

[00:20:04.570–00:20:06.730] **SPEECH**
 So now we are inside the source folder,

[00:20:06.730–00:20:10.370] **SPEECH**
 we can invoke Java virtual machine like this. We type Java

[00:20:10.370–00:20:14.910] **SPEECH**
 and then we type the full path to our main class file. What do I mean

[00:20:14.910–00:20:18.550] **SPEECH**
 by that? Well, earlier we defined this package

[00:20:18.550–00:20:23.030] **SPEECH**
 com.codewithmosh, and this class, the main class, is part

[00:20:23.030–00:20:26.850] **SPEECH**
 of this package. So, the full path to this class

[00:20:26.850–00:20:32.890] **SPEECH**
 is com.codewithmosh.main

[00:20:32.890–00:20:34.870] **SPEECH**
 Make sure to use a capital M

[00:20:34.870–00:20:38.790] **SPEECH**
 here because this is case sensitive. Now when we press enter,

[00:20:39.590–00:20:43.010] **SPEECH**
 java will look at this folder, com, inside this folder, it will

[00:20:43.010–00:20:47.130] **SPEECH**
 look at this other folder, code with mosh, and then it will find main.class

[00:20:47.130–00:20:51.090] **SPEECH**
 in that folder. It will load the bytecode and convert it to

[00:20:51.090–00:20:55.170] **SPEECH**
 the native code for the operating system we are using. So, take a look,

[00:20:55.550–00:20:59.290] **SPEECH**
 so it executed our program, hello world, beautiful. When we run

[00:20:59.290–00:21:02.730] **SPEECH**
 a program using IntelliJ, all these steps are hidden from us.

[00:21:02.730–00:21:05.270] **SPEECH**
 We don't see the compilation or execution steps.

[00:21:09.020–00:21:10.600] **SPEECH**
 So you have seen Java in action.

[00:21:11.180–00:21:13.980] **SPEECH**
 Now let me tell you five interesting facts about Java.

[00:21:14.840–00:21:19.820] **SPEECH**
 Java was developed by James Gosling in 1995 at Sun Microsystems,

[00:21:19.820–00:21:22.460] **SPEECH**
 which was later acquired by Oracle in 2010.

[00:21:22.960–00:21:27.680] **SPEECH**
 It was originally called Oak after an Oak tree that stood outside Gosling's office.

[00:21:28.220–00:21:31.340] **SPEECH**
 Later it was renamed to Green and was finally renamed to Java,

[00:21:31.540–00:21:33.020] **SPEECH**
 inspired by Java Coffee.

[00:21:33.020–00:21:35.320] **SPEECH**
 That's why its logo looks like this.

[00:21:35.860–00:21:39.440] **SPEECH**
 We have four editions of Java for building different kinds of applications.

[00:21:40.000–00:21:41.580] **SPEECH**
 We have Java Standard Edition.

[00:21:42.060–00:21:44.940] **SPEECH**
 This is the core Java platform, which is what we're using in this course.

[00:21:45.500–00:21:49.180] **SPEECH**
 It contains all of the libraries that every Java developer must learn.

[00:21:49.720–00:21:51.300] **SPEECH**
 We have Java Enterprise Edition,

[00:21:51.680–00:21:55.620] **SPEECH**
 which is used for building very large scale and distributed systems.

[00:21:55.980–00:22:01.440] **SPEECH**
 It's built on top of Java Standard Edition and provides additional libraries for building

[00:22:01.440–00:22:04.800] **SPEECH**
 fault-tolerant distributed multi-tier software.

[00:22:05.500–00:22:09.540] **SPEECH**
 We have Java Micro Edition, which is a subset of Java Standard Edition

[00:22:09.540–00:22:13.520] **SPEECH**
 designed for mobile devices. So it has libraries specific

[00:22:13.520–00:22:17.500] **SPEECH**
 to mobile devices. And finally we have Java Card, which is used

[00:22:17.500–00:22:21.500] **SPEECH**
 in smart cards. The latest version of Java is Java

[00:22:21.500–00:22:25.500] **SPEECH**
 Standard Edition 12, which was released just a few months ago in March

[00:22:25.500–00:22:29.180] **SPEECH**
 2019. Java has close to 9 million developers

[00:22:29.180–00:22:32.980] **SPEECH**
 currently about 3 billion mobile phones run Java

[00:22:32.980–00:22:37.580] **SPEECH**
 as well as 120 million TV sets and every Blu-ray player

[00:22:37.580–00:22:41.280] **SPEECH**
 According to indeed.com, the average salary of a Java

[00:22:41.280–00:22:45.300] **SPEECH**
 developer is just over $100,000 per year in the US

[00:22:45.300–00:22:49.460] **SPEECH**
 So as you can see, Java is everywhere, which means more opportunities for you

[00:22:49.460–00:22:55.950] **SPEECH**
 to get hired as a professional programmer. Now let me give you a

[00:22:55.950–00:23:00.010] **SPEECH**
 quick overview of how I've structured this course so you can get the most out of it

[00:23:00.010–00:23:03.550] **SPEECH**
 This course is the first part of my complete four part

[00:23:03.550–00:23:07.990] **SPEECH**
 Java series. Each part is about three to four hours long, so you can easily

[00:23:07.990–00:23:11.910] **SPEECH**
 complete it in a day or two. In the first part, which is what you're watching,

[00:23:12.330–00:23:16.090] **SPEECH**
 you're going to learn the fundamentals of programming with Java. In the next

[00:23:16.090–00:23:20.110] **SPEECH**
 section, you'll learn about the type system in Java, you'll learn how to work with various

[00:23:20.110–00:23:23.710] **SPEECH**
 types, such as numbers, strings, booleans, and arrays.

[00:23:24.330–00:23:28.110] **SPEECH**
 By the end of this section, you will build a mortgage calculator as your first

[00:23:28.110–00:23:32.230] **SPEECH**
 Java project. We'll be improving this calculator bit by bit throughout

[00:23:32.230–00:23:36.290] **SPEECH**
 this course. Next you will learn about control flow statements that are

[00:23:36.290–00:23:39.970] **SPEECH**
 used to build algorithms. We'll be talking about various types of

[00:23:39.970–00:23:44.390] **SPEECH**
 conditional statements and loops. Later in this section we'll add data validation

[00:23:44.390–00:23:47.990] **SPEECH**
 to our mortgage calculator to force the user to enter

[00:23:47.990–00:23:52.150] **SPEECH**
 valid values. At this point you'll be able to build basic algorithms

[00:23:52.150–00:23:56.090] **SPEECH**
 and that's great, but being a good programmer requires knowing how to write

[00:23:56.090–00:24:00.450] **SPEECH**
 code. Code that is clean and expressive. That's what separates

[00:24:00.450–00:24:04.310] **SPEECH**
 an outstanding programmer from an average programmer. So in the following

[00:24:04.310–00:24:07.770] **SPEECH**
 section we'll talk about clean coding. I will show you various techniques

[00:24:07.770–00:24:11.970] **SPEECH**
 that professional programmers use to make their code clean and maintainable.

[00:24:12.550–00:24:16.350] **SPEECH**
 And finally in the last section, you will learn how to find and fix errors

[00:24:16.350–00:24:20.130] **SPEECH**
 in your Java programs, as well as how to package them for deployment

[00:24:20.130–00:24:23.910] **SPEECH**
 so others can use them. So the materials in the first part

[00:24:23.910–00:24:28.010] **SPEECH**
 will give you a solid foundation on how to start programming in

[00:24:28.010–00:24:32.150] **SPEECH**
 Java. In the second part, we'll talk about object oriented programming

[00:24:32.150–00:24:35.850] **SPEECH**
 which is a style of programming used in most if not all Java

[00:24:35.850–00:24:39.670] **SPEECH**
 applications. Whether you want to use Java to build web, mobile,

[00:24:40.090–00:24:43.870] **SPEECH**
 or desktop applications, you need to understand object oriented programming well

[00:24:43.870–00:24:48.030] **SPEECH**
 because otherwise you're going to constantly hit obstacles. In the third part

[00:24:48.030–00:24:51.570] **SPEECH**
 we're going to talk about core Java APIs or application

[00:24:51.570–00:24:55.830] **SPEECH**
 programming interfaces, you'll learn about many of the useful classes in the

[00:24:55.830–00:25:00.030] **SPEECH**
 standard Java library. And finally, in the last part, we'll be looking at

[00:25:00.030–00:25:03.410] **SPEECH**
 the advanced features in Java, such as streams, threads,

[00:25:03.910–00:25:07.730] **SPEECH**
 database programming, and so on. So, I hope you're going to join me on this journey

[00:25:07.730–00:25:11.530] **SPEECH**
 at Master Java, the most popular programming language behind

[00:25:11.530–00:25:26.440] **SPEECH**
 millions of apps and websites. In this section, we're going to look at the fundamentals

[00:25:26.440–00:25:30.380] **SPEECH**
 of programming in Java. You're going to learn about variables and

[00:25:30.380–00:25:35.540] **SPEECH**
 constants, primitive and reference types, you're going to learn about casting or type conversion,

[00:25:36.380–00:25:41.140] **SPEECH**
 you will learn how to work with numbers, strings, and arrays, and how to read input from the user.

[00:25:41.740–00:25:45.220] **SPEECH**
 Once you learn all this, I'm going to give you a project, you're going to build a mortgage

[00:25:45.220–00:25:49.500] **SPEECH**
 calculator on your own. So make sure to pay great attention to all the materials you're going to

[00:25:49.500–00:25:54.300] **SPEECH**
 learn, because you're going to use most of them in this project. Are you ready? Now let's jump

[00:25:54.300–00:26:04.990] **SPEECH**
 in and get started. In this tutorial, we're going to talk about variables in Java. We use

[00:26:04.990–00:26:09.410] **SPEECH**
 to temporarily store data in computer's memory. Here is an example.

[00:26:10.090–00:26:13.230] **SPEECH**
 Imagine in this program you want to store someone's age in the memory.

[00:26:13.910–00:26:17.090] **SPEECH**
 So we declare a variable like this, int, age,

[00:26:17.430–00:26:21.390] **SPEECH**
 equals 30. So int or integer is the type

[00:26:21.390–00:26:25.130] **SPEECH**
 of this variable, so in this variable we can only store integers

[00:26:25.130–00:26:29.330] **SPEECH**
 which are whole numbers, like 1, 2, 3, 4, numbers that don't have a decimal

[00:26:29.330–00:26:33.890] **SPEECH**
 point. Now in Java we have several different types, I'm going to talk about them in the next tutorial.

[00:26:33.890–00:26:38.310] **SPEECH**
 So first we specify the type of our variable, then we give it a name

[00:26:38.310–00:26:42.330] **SPEECH**
 or a label, this is also called an identifier, because we use it

[00:26:42.330–00:26:45.950] **SPEECH**
 to identify our variable. This equal sign is called

[00:26:45.950–00:26:50.410] **SPEECH**
 the assignment operator, and 30 is the initial value that we are assigning

[00:26:50.410–00:26:54.090] **SPEECH**
 to this variable. So we say on line 6, we are initializing

[00:26:54.570–00:26:57.790] **SPEECH**
 this variable, which means we are giving it an initial value.

[00:26:58.150–00:27:01.470] **SPEECH**
 We always have to initialize our variables before reading them.

[00:27:01.470–00:27:05.470] **SPEECH**
 So with this line, we're storing number 30 somewhere in computer's memory

[00:27:05.470–00:27:09.690] **SPEECH**
 and we're assigning this label to that memory location. Now

[00:27:09.690–00:27:13.110] **SPEECH**
 on line 7, instead of printing hello world, we can print

[00:27:13.110–00:27:17.710] **SPEECH**
 the value of the age variable. Take a look. So I'm going to run this program

[00:27:17.710–00:27:23.240] **SPEECH**
 using control and r, there you go. Now we see 30

[00:27:23.240–00:27:27.140] **SPEECH**
 on the terminal. Beautiful. We can also change the value of our variable

[00:27:27.140–00:27:31.300] **SPEECH**
 so after we initialize it, perhaps we can change it to

[00:27:31.300–00:27:37.070] **SPEECH**
 35, now when we run this program again, we see 35.

[00:27:37.610–00:27:40.630] **SPEECH**
 Beautiful. We can also initialize multiple

[00:27:40.630–00:27:44.790] **SPEECH**
 variables on the same line, but this is something that I don't recommend, because it makes

[00:27:44.790–00:27:48.710] **SPEECH**
 your code ugly and hard to read. Here is an example, we can declare

[00:27:48.710–00:27:52.610] **SPEECH**
 another variable, like temperature, and set it to 20,

[00:27:53.150–00:27:56.550] **SPEECH**
 so using a comma we can declare multiple variables

[00:27:56.550–00:28:00.670] **SPEECH**
 on the same line. Now even though this is technically possible, it's not something that

[00:28:00.670–00:28:04.750] **SPEECH**
 recommend. So it's always better to declare one

[00:28:04.750–00:28:08.870] **SPEECH**
 variable on each line, like this. We can also copy the

[00:28:08.870–00:28:12.530] **SPEECH**
 value of one variable into another. Here is an example. Let me delete

[00:28:12.530–00:28:16.470] **SPEECH**
 these variables, and declare a new variable called my age,

[00:28:16.950–00:28:20.490] **SPEECH**
 we set it to 30, and then we declare another variable like

[00:28:20.490–00:28:24.790] **SPEECH**
 her age, and we set it to my age. So now

[00:28:24.790–00:28:28.110] **SPEECH**
 when we print her age, we're going to see

[00:28:28.770–00:28:32.210] **SPEECH**
 take a look. So,

[00:28:32.670–00:28:36.430] **SPEECH**
 on line 7, we're copying the value of this variable into this

[00:28:36.430–00:28:40.310] **SPEECH**
 other variable. Now one thing I want you to pay attention to here is the

[00:28:40.310–00:28:44.130] **SPEECH**
 convention I have used for naming our variables. As I told you before,

[00:28:44.390–00:28:48.050] **SPEECH**
 this is called the camel case notation. So we should capitalize

[00:28:48.050–00:28:51.850] **SPEECH**
 the first letter of every word except the first word.

[00:28:52.310–00:28:56.510] **SPEECH**
 So in this case, the first word, my, it's all in lowercase, but the second word

[00:28:56.510–00:29:00.710] **SPEECH**
 starts with a capital letter. So this is all about declaring and

[00:29:00.710–00:29:04.630] **SPEECH**
 initializing variables. In the next tutorial we're going to talk about various types

[00:29:04.630–00:29:14.150] **SPEECH**
 in Java. In this tutorial we're going to talk about various types in

[00:29:14.150–00:29:18.290] **SPEECH**
 Java. Basically we have two categories of types. We have primitive

[00:29:18.290–00:29:22.110] **SPEECH**
 types for storing simple values, and non-primitive types or

[00:29:22.110–00:29:26.290] **SPEECH**
 reference types for storing complex objects. So in the category

[00:29:26.290–00:29:29.970] **SPEECH**
 of primitive types we have byte, which takes one byte of memory,

[00:29:29.970–00:29:33.550] **SPEECH**
 and in one byte we can store values from 128

[00:29:33.550–00:29:38.150] **SPEECH**
 to 127. Now the more bytes we have, the larger

[00:29:38.150–00:29:41.970] **SPEECH**
 numbers we can store. So next we have short which takes 2 bytes

[00:29:41.970–00:29:46.110] **SPEECH**
 and with this we can store values up to 32,000. Next we have

[00:29:46.110–00:29:50.090] **SPEECH**
 integer, which we have seen before, integers take 4 bytes of memory and

[00:29:50.090–00:29:53.830] **SPEECH**
 allow us to store values up to 2 billion. Then we have long

[00:29:53.830–00:29:57.430] **SPEECH**
 which takes 8 bytes, and with this we can store even larger numbers.

[00:29:57.430–00:30:01.530] **SPEECH**
 Now all these types are for storing whole numbers that don't have

[00:30:01.530–00:30:05.750] **SPEECH**
 a decimal point. If you want to store a number that has a decimal point, you have to use

[00:30:05.750–00:30:10.130] **SPEECH**
 float or double. Float takes 4 bytes, double takes 8 bytes.

[00:30:10.310–00:30:12.810] **SPEECH**
 So obviously with double we can store larger numbers.

[00:30:13.510–00:30:17.630] **SPEECH**
 Next we have char for storing a single character, like a, b, c, and these

[00:30:17.630–00:30:21.510] **SPEECH**
 char types take 2 bytes, so they support international letters.

[00:30:22.110–00:30:25.930] **SPEECH**
 And finally we have boolean for storing boolean values, which can be true or

[00:30:25.930–00:30:30.030] **SPEECH**
 just like yes or no in English. Now let's take a look

[00:30:30.030–00:30:34.330] **SPEECH**
 at a few examples. Earlier we used an integer for storing someone's

[00:30:34.330–00:30:38.270] **SPEECH**
 age, but as you learned, integers take 4 bytes and allow us

[00:30:38.270–00:30:42.470] **SPEECH**
 to store values up to 2 billion. We don't need 4 bytes of memory to store

[00:30:42.470–00:30:46.470] **SPEECH**
 someone's age. All we need is 1 byte, because with 1 byte we can store

[00:30:46.470–00:30:50.370] **SPEECH**
 values up to 127. So I'm going to change this

[00:30:50.370–00:30:54.210] **SPEECH**
 to byte, that is better. Now let's look at another example.

[00:30:54.210–00:30:58.330] **SPEECH**
 let's say we want to store the number of times a YouTube video has been watched.

[00:30:58.950–00:31:01.750] **SPEECH**
 So we define an integer called views count,

[00:31:02.370–00:31:06.190] **SPEECH**
 note that I'm always using meaningful names for my variables, because these names

[00:31:06.190–00:31:10.330] **SPEECH**
 help us understand what this code does. I've seen some people use variable names

[00:31:10.330–00:31:14.410] **SPEECH**
 like v or v1 or n, nobody knows what these

[00:31:14.410–00:31:17.990] **SPEECH**
 variables do. So as a best practice, always use meaningful

[00:31:17.990–00:31:21.990] **SPEECH**
 and descriptive names for your variables. So views count,

[00:31:21.990–00:31:25.770] **SPEECH**
 we set this to a large number, like 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6,

[00:31:25.830–00:31:30.110] **SPEECH**
 7, 8, 9. Now, in Java, whenever you deal with a large

[00:31:30.110–00:31:33.890] **SPEECH**
 number like this, you can use an underscore to separate

[00:31:33.890–00:31:38.010] **SPEECH**
 every 3 digits. Just like how we use a comma in our documents

[00:31:38.830–00:31:42.070] **SPEECH**
 to make our numbers more readable, we can use an underscore

[00:31:42.710–00:31:46.550] **SPEECH**
 in Java. So, with integers we can store

[00:31:46.550–00:31:50.130] **SPEECH**
 values up to 2 billion. But let's say the number of times this

[00:31:50.130–00:31:53.930] **SPEECH**
 video has been watched is 3 billion. So, I add a 3 here,

[00:31:54.570–00:31:58.110] **SPEECH**
 now we have a red underline that indicates an error, let me hover our

[00:31:58.110–00:32:01.670] **SPEECH**
 mouse over it, we see this tool tip, integer number too large.

[00:32:02.210–00:32:06.170] **SPEECH**
 So, we need to change the type of this variable to long, however

[00:32:06.170–00:32:10.210] **SPEECH**
 the error is still there. What's going on here? The reason we're

[00:32:10.210–00:32:13.850] **SPEECH**
 getting this error is that by default, java sees these numbers as integers

[00:32:13.850–00:32:18.290] **SPEECH**
 so even though we have defined the type of this variable as long, java compiler

[00:32:18.290–00:32:22.430] **SPEECH**
 sees this value as an integer, and he thinks this value is too large for

[00:32:22.430–00:32:26.470] **SPEECH**
 an integer. To solve this problem, we need to add an l as

[00:32:26.470–00:32:30.630] **SPEECH**
 a suffix to this number. We can use an uppercase or a lowercase l.

[00:32:31.050–00:32:34.030] **SPEECH**
 But as you can see, a lowercase l kind of looks like a one, so

[00:32:34.030–00:32:38.450] **SPEECH**
 it's better to use a capital L. So these are examples of

[00:32:38.450–00:32:42.310] **SPEECH**
 whole numbers. Now let's declare a variable for storing a number with a decimal

[00:32:42.310–00:32:46.430] **SPEECH**
 point. So double price, we set this to 10,

[00:32:47.610–00:32:50.790] **SPEECH**
 Obviously, the double variable is too large for storing

[00:32:50.790–00:32:53.510] **SPEECH**
 the price of a product, so we can change this to float,

[00:32:54.370–00:32:58.170] **SPEECH**
 that is better, but we have a compilation error here, take a look.

[00:32:59.070–00:33:02.750] **SPEECH**
 Incompatible types, required float, but found double.

[00:33:03.590–00:33:06.410] **SPEECH**
 The reason we are seeing this error is that by default, Java sees

[00:33:06.410–00:33:10.790] **SPEECH**
 these numbers with a decimal point as double, so even though

[00:33:10.790–00:33:14.970] **SPEECH**
 we set the type of this variable to float, Java sees this number as a double.

[00:33:15.530–00:33:19.030] **SPEECH**
 So, just like how we added a suffix to this number

[00:33:19.030–00:33:23.190] **SPEECH**
 to represent it as a long, we need to add a suffix here to represent this

[00:33:23.190–00:33:27.150] **SPEECH**
 number as a float, and that suffix is f. Once again, we can

[00:33:27.150–00:33:31.150] **SPEECH**
 use an uppercase or lowercase f. So,

[00:33:31.490–00:33:34.870] **SPEECH**
 these are examples of numbers, now, let's store a character,

[00:33:35.250–00:33:39.230] **SPEECH**
 so char, we call it letter, and we set it to

[00:33:39.230–00:33:43.210] **SPEECH**
 a. Note that we should always surround single characters with single

[00:33:43.210–00:33:47.550] **SPEECH**
 quotes and multiple characters or strings with double quotes.

[00:33:48.150–00:33:50.910] **SPEECH**
 Ok, so char represents only one character,

[00:33:51.650–00:33:55.310] **SPEECH**
 string represents a series of characters. And finally let's see an

[00:33:55.310–00:33:58.730] **SPEECH**
 example of a boolean. So we define a boolean variable

[00:33:58.730–00:34:02.850] **SPEECH**
 called is eligible. Is this person eligible for

[00:34:02.850–00:34:07.430] **SPEECH**
 loan or not. We set this to true or false.

[00:34:08.010–00:34:11.210] **SPEECH**
 These are the boolean values. Now note that all these words

[00:34:11.210–00:34:15.370] **SPEECH**
 coded in orange are reserved keywords in java, just

[00:34:15.370–00:34:19.590] **SPEECH**
 like public, static void, class, package, these are all

[00:34:19.590–00:34:23.310] **SPEECH**
 reserved keywords. So we cannot use these reserved keywords, the name are

[00:34:23.310–00:34:32.660] **SPEECH**
 variables, classes, and methods. In the last tutorial, you learned

[00:34:32.660–00:34:36.360] **SPEECH**
 that we use primitive types to store simple values like numbers,

[00:34:36.920–00:34:40.340] **SPEECH**
 boolean values, or single characters. In contrast,

[00:34:40.760–00:34:44.820] **SPEECH**
 we use reference types to store complex objects, like data objects,

[00:34:44.820–00:34:49.060] **SPEECH**
 or mail messages, these are complex objects. Now in

[00:34:49.060–00:34:52.900] **SPEECH**
 java we have 8 primitive types that you have seen before, all the other

[00:34:52.900–00:34:55.560] **SPEECH**
 types are reference types. Let me show you an example.

[00:34:57.140–00:35:00.420] **SPEECH**
 So, here in this program, first I'm going to declare a primitive type,

[00:35:01.040–00:35:04.500] **SPEECH**
 let's say byte h equals 30.

[00:35:05.540–00:35:08.760] **SPEECH**
 Now, declaring and initializing a reference type is slightly

[00:35:08.760–00:35:12.700] **SPEECH**
 different from a primitive type. Let me show you. So, let's type

[00:35:12.700–00:35:16.460] **SPEECH**
 date. Now here in this tooltip box, which is called

[00:35:16.460–00:35:20.560] **SPEECH**
 IntelliSense, we can see various classes that have date in their name.

[00:35:20.880–00:35:24.980] **SPEECH**
 So IntelliJ is helping us complete our code by suggesting these class names.

[00:35:25.960–00:35:28.200] **SPEECH**
 Now here we have a date class in this package,

[00:35:28.720–00:35:32.740] **SPEECH**
 java.util, so this package contains a lot of utility classes

[00:35:32.740–00:35:36.960] **SPEECH**
 that are useful in a lot of programs. You also have a date class in a different

[00:35:36.960–00:35:40.920] **SPEECH**
 package, java.sql or SQL, which is used for

[00:35:40.920–00:35:45.480] **SPEECH**
 programming databases. So this is the benefit of packages. We can have the same

[00:35:45.480–00:35:49.420] **SPEECH**
 class, but in different packages, they don't conflict. So packages

[00:35:49.420–00:35:53.340] **SPEECH**
 create a name space for our classes. Now in this case,

[00:35:54.040–00:35:57.380] **SPEECH**
 if we select the first date class, and press enter or

[00:35:57.380–00:36:01.280] **SPEECH**
 tab, IntelliJ automatically adds this

[00:36:01.280–00:36:05.200] **SPEECH**
 line for us. Import java.util.date.

[00:36:06.420–00:36:08.900] **SPEECH**
 So because currently we are in this package,

[00:36:08.900–00:36:12.760] **SPEECH**
 in order to use a class from a different package, we need to import it.

[00:36:13.320–00:36:17.200] **SPEECH**
 So here we are importing the date class in this package. We'll talk about

[00:36:17.200–00:36:21.280] **SPEECH**
 packages in more detail in the future. So, back to our date variable.

[00:36:22.120–00:36:24.760] **SPEECH**
 Let's give this variable a name, like now.

[00:36:25.660–00:36:29.040] **SPEECH**
 Now, we set this, here we need to use the new operator

[00:36:29.040–00:36:33.080] **SPEECH**
 to allocate memory for this variable, and this is one of the differences between

[00:36:33.080–00:36:37.120] **SPEECH**
 the primitive and reference types. When declaring primitive types, we don't need to

[00:36:37.120–00:36:41.420] **SPEECH**
 memory. Memory is allocated and released by Java runtime environment.

[00:36:42.200–00:36:45.200] **SPEECH**
 But when dealing with reference types, we should always allocate memory.

[00:36:45.620–00:36:49.540] **SPEECH**
 Now we don't have to release this memory, Java runtime environment will automatically take

[00:36:49.540–00:36:53.360] **SPEECH**
 care of that. So, we use the new operator, and then repeat

[00:36:53.360–00:36:57.040] **SPEECH**
 the name of our class, in this case date, and then we add

[00:36:57.040–00:37:01.140] **SPEECH**
 parenthesis, followed by a semicolon. In this example,

[00:37:01.660–00:37:05.600] **SPEECH**
 this variable we have defined here is an instance of the date class.

[00:37:05.600–00:37:10.120] **SPEECH**
 So these classes define templates or blueprints for creating new

[00:37:10.120–00:37:14.160] **SPEECH**
 objects, new instances. As another example, we can have a class

[00:37:14.160–00:37:17.840] **SPEECH**
 called human, and we can have objects like John, Bob,

[00:37:18.320–00:37:21.600] **SPEECH**
 Mary, and so on. So an object is an instance of a class.

[00:37:22.760–00:37:25.860] **SPEECH**
 Now, these objects or these classes have members that we can

[00:37:25.860–00:37:29.580] **SPEECH**
 access using the dot operator. So we can type now,

[00:37:29.900–00:37:34.020] **SPEECH**
 dot, and these are all the members defined in this class, or in this object.

[00:37:34.020–00:37:38.200] **SPEECH**
 For example, we have a method called get time, and this

[00:37:38.200–00:37:42.080] **SPEECH**
 returns the time component of this object. This is another difference between

[00:37:42.080–00:37:45.940] **SPEECH**
 primitive types and reference types. These primitive types don't have members.

[00:37:46.640–00:37:49.960] **SPEECH**
 So if you type age dot, we don't see anything. These

[00:37:49.960–00:37:54.220] **SPEECH**
 items you see here are not members of age, they're code snippets which allow us to

[00:37:54.220–00:37:57.920] **SPEECH**
 quickly generate code. For example, we can select 4i,

[00:37:58.260–00:38:02.080] **SPEECH**
 and this automatically generates this block of code for us. We'll talk about this in the future.

[00:38:02.680–00:38:06.340] **SPEECH**
 So, this age variable is a primitive type, it's not

[00:38:06.340–00:38:10.300] **SPEECH**
 an object, it doesn't have any members, and that's why when we use the

[00:38:10.300–00:38:13.860] **SPEECH**
 dot operator, we don't see anything here. Now let's delete this line,

[00:38:14.180–00:38:18.360] **SPEECH**
 and instead print the value of this data object. So once again we can type

[00:38:18.360–00:38:22.600] **SPEECH**
 system, this is a class, so we can use the dot operator to access

[00:38:22.600–00:38:26.460] **SPEECH**
 its members, here we have out, which is a field, and the type

[00:38:26.460–00:38:30.340] **SPEECH**
 of this field is print string, which is another class in Java.

[00:38:30.340–00:38:34.580] **SPEECH**
 So once again we can use the dot operator, and call the print line

[00:38:34.580–00:38:38.680] **SPEECH**
 function. Now let me show you a very cool shortcut. Instead of typing all this

[00:38:38.680–00:38:41.780] **SPEECH**
 we can use one of these code snippets. So we type

[00:38:41.780–00:38:46.300] **SPEECH**
 S-O-U-T, and press tab, and this generates

[00:38:46.300–00:38:50.020] **SPEECH**
 this piece of code for us. Alright, now let's pass our data object here.

[00:38:50.720–00:38:54.060] **SPEECH**
 Note that I have not surrounded this variable with double quotes,

[00:38:54.380–00:38:58.660] **SPEECH**
 because this is a string, and if we run this program, we'll see now on the terminal.

[00:38:58.660–00:39:03.000] **SPEECH**
 there you go. We don't want this. You want the value of our data object,

[00:39:03.280–00:39:06.120] **SPEECH**
 not a label. So, let's remove

[00:39:07.200–00:39:09.480] **SPEECH**
 the quotes and run the program again.

[00:39:10.820–00:39:20.260] **SPEECH**
 So here's the current date time on my machine. You have learned a little bit about

[00:39:20.260–00:39:24.200] **SPEECH**
 the differences between the primitive and reference types. So you know that we

[00:39:24.200–00:39:28.120] **SPEECH**
 use primitive types for storing simple values, and reference types

[00:39:28.120–00:39:32.140] **SPEECH**
 for storing complex objects. But there is a very important difference between

[00:39:32.140–00:39:36.280] **SPEECH**
 these two categories of types in terms of memory management. And

[00:39:36.280–00:39:40.200] **SPEECH**
 that's what we're going to talk about in this tutorial. So, I'm going to declare a primitive

[00:39:40.200–00:39:44.300] **SPEECH**
 variable, x, and set it to one, and then I'm going to

[00:39:44.300–00:39:48.160] **SPEECH**
 declare another variable like y, and set it to

[00:39:48.160–00:39:52.180] **SPEECH**
 x. So in this example, we have two different variables, x

[00:39:52.180–00:39:56.080] **SPEECH**
 and y, and these two variables are at different memory locations.

[00:39:56.500–00:39:59.980] **SPEECH**
 So they're completely independent of each other. In other words,

[00:39:59.980–00:40:03.900] **SPEECH**
 if I change the value of x, y is not going to get affected.

[00:40:04.200–00:40:08.340] **SPEECH**
 Let me show you. So I'm going to update x to 2, and then print

[00:40:08.340–00:40:11.840] **SPEECH**
 y. So s-y-u-t, tab, y.

[00:40:12.700–00:40:14.440] **SPEECH**
 Let's take a look. So run,

[00:40:15.840–00:40:19.520] **SPEECH**
 as you can see, y is not affected because x and y are completely

[00:40:19.520–00:40:23.880] **SPEECH**
 independent of each other. However, when we use a reference type,

[00:40:24.200–00:40:27.780] **SPEECH**
 this behavior is different. Let's take a look. So, I'm going to delete

[00:40:27.780–00:40:34.380] **SPEECH**
 all the code here. In java we have a point class that is defined

[00:40:34.380–00:40:38.240] **SPEECH**
 in this package, java.awt. So

[00:40:38.240–00:40:42.620] **SPEECH**
 you press enter, and now we have this import statement on the top

[00:40:42.620–00:40:47.120] **SPEECH**
 beautiful. Let's declare a variable like point 1 and set it to

[00:40:47.120–00:40:50.400] **SPEECH**
 new point. Here we can pass

[00:40:50.400–00:40:53.980] **SPEECH**
 the initial values for x and y. So I'm going to pass

[00:40:53.980–00:40:58.340] **SPEECH**
 1 and 1. So IntelliJ automatically adds

[00:40:58.340–00:41:02.580] **SPEECH**
 these labels, x and y. Now, just like before,

[00:41:02.800–00:41:05.580] **SPEECH**
 I'm going to declare another variable, point two,

[00:41:06.300–00:41:10.380] **SPEECH**
 and set it to point one. And this is where things get interesting.

[00:41:11.100–00:41:13.740] **SPEECH**
 When java runtime environment executes line eight,

[00:41:14.460–00:41:18.320] **SPEECH**
 first it's going to allocate some memory to store this point object.

[00:41:18.980–00:41:21.860] **SPEECH**
 Let's see, the address of that memory location is 100.

[00:41:22.700–00:41:26.300] **SPEECH**
 Then it's going to allocate a separate part of the memory, and

[00:41:26.300–00:41:30.160] **SPEECH**
 it's going to attach this label to that memory location, point 1.

[00:41:30.920–00:41:34.100] **SPEECH**
 In that memory location, it's going to store the address

 over here, let's take a look. Number format is abstract, it cannot

 be instantiated. So in Java we have this concept of abstract

[01:20:56.460–01:21:00.480] **SPEECH**
 classes, so some classes are abstract and they are basically like a half baked

[01:21:00.480–01:21:04.080] **SPEECH**

`````text
How to Calculate Mortgage Pa
https://www.wikihow.com/Calculate-Mortgage-Payments
wikiHow to do anything...
EDIT
HELP US
EXPLORE
LOG IN
MESSAGES

Method 2
Calculating Mortgage Payments with an Equation
• P is your principal.
• r is your monthly interest rate, calculated by dividing your annual interest rate by 12.
• n is your number of payments (the number of months you will be paying the loan)
[6]

M =?
P =$100,000
r =0.005
n =180
M = P $\frac{r(1+r)^{n}}{(1+r)^{n}-1}$
`````

[01:46:04.220–01:46:07.780] **SPEECH**

[01:46:09.020–01:46:12.140] **SPEECH**

[01:46:17.240–01:46:20.400] **SPEECH**
 drink, plenty of water. Otherwise, if it's

[01:46:20.400–01:46:24.240] **SPEECH**
 between 20 and 30, you want to print it's a nice day, and

[01:46:24.240–01:46:28.380] **SPEECH**
 otherwise we want to print it's a cold day. So let's see how we can implement

[01:46:28.380–01:46:32.160] **SPEECH**

 we start by declaring a variable, temperature,

 we set it to 32. Now we use an if statement,

[01:46:39.860–01:46:43.780] **SPEECH**
 followed by a pair of parenthesis. Inside this parenthesis we type

[01:46:43.780–01:46:48.100] **SPEECH**
 a boolean expression or a boolean value. So, let's say temp

[01:46:48.100–01:46:51.400] **SPEECH**
 is greater than 30. Now,

 if this condition is true, the statement that we add after this if statement

[01:46:56.120–01:47:00.040] **SPEECH**
 will be executed. So let's print, it's a

[01:47:00.040–01:47:02.980] **SPEECH**
 hot day. Let's run the program,

 we get this message because temperature is greater than 30.

[01:47:07.540–01:47:11.560] **SPEECH**

 So this is the basic structure for a for loop.

 Now, here we can repeat one or more statements using this for loop.

 so we can add our hello world message here,

[02:07:31.470–02:07:35.250] **SPEECH**
 like here we have a single statement so we don't need braces, but if you have multiple

 Now here's one thing you need to remember, if you want to execute something 5 times,

 2, 1. So this is all about 4 loops. Next, we're going to

 in this tutorial we're going to talk about y loops in java. y loops are

 terms of syntax. Let me show you. So we're going to continue with the example from the last

[02:10:08.940–02:10:13.100] **SPEECH**
 tutorial. I'm going to rewrite this code using a y loop. So

[02:10:13.100–02:10:16.920] **SPEECH**
 first we declare a loop variable, and i,

[02:10:17.100–02:10:21.180] **SPEECH**
 we initialize it to zero. Next we type while, and here

[02:10:21.180–02:10:25.080] **SPEECH**
 in parenthesis we type our loop condition. While i

[02:10:25.080–02:10:29.000] **SPEECH**
 greater than zero, then we're going to execute the code

 inside this block. So I'm going to copy this from here,

 paste it in this block, and find out if we need to decrement i

[02:10:38.100–02:10:41.380] **SPEECH**
 like this. So at the end of each iteration

 we decrement i just like our for loops. So as we can

[02:10:45.340–02:10:49.120] **SPEECH**
 see we can achieve the same thing using a for loop or a y loop.

[02:10:49.740–02:10:53.240] **SPEECH**
 However, the implementation using the for loop is a little bit lighter

[02:10:53.240–02:10:57.540] **SPEECH**
 and cleaner. So in situations where you know ahead of time

[02:10:57.540–02:11:00.800] **SPEECH**
 how many times you want to execute one or more statements,

[02:11:01.520–02:11:05.180] **SPEECH**
 it's better to use a for loop. Why loops are better in situations where

[02:11:05.180–02:11:09.360] **SPEECH**
 we don't know exactly how many times we want to repeat something. For example,

[02:11:09.860–02:11:13.240] **SPEECH**
 let's say we're going to write a program and ask the user to continuously

[02:11:13.240–02:11:17.080] **SPEECH**
 enter something until they type quit. The moment they type quit,

[02:11:17.360–02:11:20.880] **SPEECH**
 we're going to terminate the program. In that situation, we don't know how many times

[02:11:20.880–02:11:24.960] **SPEECH**
 the user is going to enter something. So let's write that program using

[02:11:24.960–02:11:30.960] **SPEECH**
 a while loop. I'm going to delete everything from here. Alright, we're going to start

[02:11:30.960–02:11:35.040] **SPEECH**
 with our while loop. Now what is our loop condition here? We don't have

[02:11:35.040–02:11:38.980] **SPEECH**
 a counter variable in this example. Instead we want to check to see if the

[02:11:38.980–02:11:42.760] **SPEECH**
 user entered quit or not. So here we can declare

[02:11:42.760–02:11:46.920] **SPEECH**
 a string called input and initialize it to an

[02:11:46.920–02:11:50.700] **SPEECH**
 empty string. Then we can write a while loop like this.

[02:11:50.700–02:11:54.320] **SPEECH**
 While input does not equal to quit,

[02:11:55.180–02:11:59.220] **SPEECH**
 now this code is not going to work because input is a string, which is a reference type,

[02:11:59.820–02:12:03.120] **SPEECH**
 and we cannot use comparison operators between reference

[02:12:03.120–02:12:07.280] **SPEECH**
 types. Because these operators will compare the address of our string objects,

[02:12:07.520–02:12:10.880] **SPEECH**
 not their value. So if you have two strings, quit and quit,

[02:12:11.160–02:12:15.140] **SPEECH**
 but stored in different memory locations, they have different addresses. So

[02:12:15.140–02:12:18.360] **SPEECH**
 we cannot use the inequality operator to compare their value.

[02:12:18.960–02:12:22.220] **SPEECH**
 instead we need to use the equals method

[02:12:22.220–02:12:26.380] **SPEECH**
 of string objects. So we want to check to see if the input

[02:12:26.380–02:12:30.180] **SPEECH**
 equals quit. Now here we need to apply the not operator

[02:12:30.180–02:12:34.400] **SPEECH**
 so as long as the input does not equal quit, we're going to

[02:12:34.400–02:12:38.180] **SPEECH**
 continuously ask the user to enter something. So here we can print

[02:12:39.320–02:12:42.480] **SPEECH**
 a label like input, and then

[02:12:42.480–02:12:46.240] **SPEECH**
 we can use a scanner object to read something from the terminal. So

[02:12:46.240–02:12:50.420] **SPEECH**
 let's create a scanner object, and instantiate it

[02:12:50.420–02:12:54.560] **SPEECH**
 using system.in, and then

[02:12:54.560–02:12:58.540] **SPEECH**
 call scanner.next, this will return a string

[02:12:58.540–02:13:02.240] **SPEECH**
 so we can store it in this input variable. Now with this

[02:13:02.240–02:13:06.100] **SPEECH**
 implementation, in every iteration, we're going to create a new

[02:13:06.100–02:13:10.260] **SPEECH**
 scanner object, so if the user enters ten numbers, we're going to create

[02:13:10.260–02:13:14.420] **SPEECH**
 ten scanner objects in memory. This is unnecessary, and it's actually

[02:13:14.420–02:13:18.580] **SPEECH**
 practice because it's going to pollute our memory. So it's better to create

[02:13:18.580–02:13:21.580] **SPEECH**
 the scanner object outside of our while loop,

[02:13:22.520–02:13:26.560] **SPEECH**
 and then simply use it here. Also, here we're assuming that

[02:13:26.560–02:13:30.300] **SPEECH**
 the user is typing everything in lowercase, so if they type quit in

[02:13:30.300–02:13:34.140] **SPEECH**
 uppercase or any combinations of lowercase and uppercase characters,

[02:13:34.580–02:13:38.480] **SPEECH**
 this logic is not going to work the way we want. So

[02:13:38.480–02:13:42.000] **SPEECH**
 over here, right after reading something from the terminal,

[02:13:42.000–02:13:45.880] **SPEECH**
 we're going to call the two lower case methods of string objects

[02:13:45.880–02:13:50.000] **SPEECH**
 to convert it to lower case. Now to make this program more interesting

[02:13:50.000–02:13:54.220] **SPEECH**
 let's echo back whatever the user enters. So we simply

[02:13:54.220–02:13:58.500] **SPEECH**
 print that on the terminal. Now let's run this and see what happens.

[02:14:01.100–02:14:03.760] **SPEECH**
 So I'm going to enter a few numbers like one, two,

[02:14:04.040–02:14:08.220] **SPEECH**
 and three, whatever we type gets echoed back. But the moment we type

[02:14:08.220–02:14:12.140] **SPEECH**
 quit, our program terminates. So

[02:14:12.140–02:14:16.200] **SPEECH**
 while loops are useful in situations where we don't know ahead of time how many times

[02:14:16.200–02:14:25.740] **SPEECH**
 we want to repeat something. In Java we have another type of loop called

[02:14:25.740–02:14:30.000] **SPEECH**
 a do-while loop. It's very similar to a while loop, but it gets executed

[02:14:30.000–02:14:33.760] **SPEECH**
 at least once. Let me show you what I mean. So I'm going to rewrite the same

[02:14:33.760–02:14:37.480] **SPEECH**
 code using a do-while loop. We start with a do keyword,

[02:14:38.340–02:14:41.600] **SPEECH**
 then we create a code block. At the end of this code

[02:14:41.600–02:14:45.640] **SPEECH**
 block, we type while followed by our

[02:14:45.640–02:14:50.080] **SPEECH**
 condition. So not input.equals

[02:14:50.080–02:14:53.260] **SPEECH**
 quit. And then we terminate this using a semicolor.

[02:14:54.040–02:14:58.060] **SPEECH**
 Now inside the body of this loop, we'll simply copy all these lines we have here.

[02:15:02.620–02:15:05.700] **SPEECH**
 Now compare these two types of loops. With while loops,

[02:15:06.140–02:15:09.640] **SPEECH**
 we check the condition first. So if the condition is false the first

[02:15:09.640–02:15:13.540] **SPEECH**
 time, this loop will never get executed. In contrast

[02:15:13.540–02:15:17.740] **SPEECH**
 with do while loops, we check the condition last. So do

[02:15:17.740–02:15:21.840] **SPEECH**
 Y loops always get executed at least once. Even if the

[02:15:21.840–02:15:25.820] **SPEECH**
 condition is false, that is the only difference. Now in reality most of the

[02:15:25.820–02:15:29.820] **SPEECH**
 time we use Y loops, do Y loops are rarely used but there are certain cases

[02:15:29.820–02:15:33.920] **SPEECH**
 for them. So just be aware of them but most of the time, prefer to use Y

[02:15:33.920–02:15:42.900] **SPEECH**
 loops. We're going to continue with the example from the last tutorial.

[02:15:43.520–02:15:46.900] **SPEECH**
 This program we have written has a tiny problem, let me show you.

[02:15:47.560–02:15:51.300] **SPEECH**
 So I'm going to run this, enter a couple numbers,

[02:15:51.300–02:15:55.220] **SPEECH**
 these numbers get echo back, beautiful. If we type quit,

[02:15:55.740–02:15:59.360] **SPEECH**
 the program terminates, but the word quit also gets echo

[02:15:59.360–02:16:03.720] **SPEECH**
 back. This is a bit weird, so let's look at a couple of ideas for solving this problem.

[02:16:04.600–02:16:07.520] **SPEECH**
 Back to our code, one way to solve this problem is

[02:16:07.520–02:16:11.660] **SPEECH**
 to check the input before printing it. So here we can type

[02:16:12.240–02:16:15.360] **SPEECH**
 an if statement, if the input does not equal

[02:16:15.360–02:16:19.400] **SPEECH**
 quit, then we print it. So, not equal

[02:16:19.400–02:16:23.460] **SPEECH**
 input dot equals quit. If

[02:16:23.460–02:16:27.620] **SPEECH**
 this condition is true, then we're going to print the input. Let's

[02:16:27.620–02:16:31.500] **SPEECH**
 take a look. So, one, two, quit,

[02:16:32.320–02:16:35.680] **SPEECH**
 beautiful. We solved the problem. There is another way to solve this problem

[02:16:35.680–02:16:39.620] **SPEECH**
 as well. Let me show you. We can reverse this condition, so

[02:16:39.620–02:16:43.540] **SPEECH**
 if the user types quit, you can immediately jump out of the loop using the

[02:16:43.540–02:16:46.640] **SPEECH**
 break statement. So I'm going to remove the not operator,

[02:16:47.140–02:16:50.240] **SPEECH**
 if the user types quit, we're going to break

[02:16:50.240–02:16:54.720] **SPEECH**
 out of the loop. Otherwise, we're going to continue execution and

[02:16:54.720–02:16:58.640] **SPEECH**
 print this input on the terminal. So when java sees the break statement
